### Changelog next version

#### Highlights

#### Changes

* **The startup warning for a MongoDB event store with `DCB` and without `STREAM` now fixes a collection that has several unusable indexes on `dcbTags`.** It names every one of them, and the commands it prints drop each unusable index and then create the sparse one, so the result is a single usable index. An index that is only hidden is unhidden instead of rebuilt. The check also accepts a partial index whose filter is an `$and` of `{ dcbTags: { $exists: true } }`, which MongoDB uses like the plain filter. The warning now says which reads the index helps (`count`, `exists`, the `wholeStoreLock()` append check and match-all reads) and doesn't promise a query plan for any of them. The check lists indexes only after it finds a stream event, so a collection without one no longer needs the `listIndexes` privilege. See [#1236](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1236).
* **A RabbitMQ source on a connection with topology recovery turned off consumes again after an automatic recovery.** It kept the tag of a consumer that no longer existed, and its poll starts a consumer only while it holds no tag, so it never consumed again. `RabbitMqCloudEventSource` and `RabbitMqDomainEventSource` now follow the recoveries of the connection they were built with. When a recovery ends without the client having registered the consumer again, the source clears the tag and the poll registers a new consumer. With topology recovery on, the source keeps the consumer the client registered. A source built on a `Connection` that wraps an `AutorecoveringConnection` can't follow its recoveries and logs a warning when it starts. See [ADR 153](doc/architecture/decisions/0153-a-rabbitmq-source-registers-a-new-consumer-when-a-recovery-leaves-it-without-one.md) and [#1066](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1066).
* **A RabbitMQ source whose cancel of its consumer fails no longer believes it is still consuming.** A cancel that threw, for example "Unknown consumerTag" after a recovery, left the tag in place and the poll never started another consumer. A cancel that fails because the channel has no such consumer, or no longer works, now counts as done. After any other failure, such as a reply that timed out, the consumer may still exist, so the next poll cancels it again before it registers another. A source that stops consuming while a recovery runs cancels the consumer the recovery registers again, instead of leaving it registered next to the one it starts when it resumes. A consumer the broker cancels, for example because its queue was deleted, is cancelled on the channel too, since the client would otherwise register it again on a recovery. A cancel that keeps failing no longer keeps the poll from releasing a held delivery. See [#1106](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1106).
* **A closed channel on the RabbitMQ publisher is replaced before the next publish.** A repair that failed to open a new channel left the closed one in place, so every later publish failed on it, also after the connection was back. `RabbitMqCloudEventSink` and `RabbitMqDomainEventSink` now check the channel before they send. A publish after `close()` on a `RabbitMqCloudEventSink` fails instead of opening a channel that nothing closes. See [#1287](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1287).
* **`close()` on a RabbitMQ source with a huge `closeTimeout` closes the source.** A timeout beyond about 292 years made `close()` throw `ArithmeticException` before it cancelled the consumer. A timeout longer than about 146 years now counts as that long. See [#1094](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1094).
* **A failure while a RabbitMQ source closes no longer replaces the one already being thrown.** A failure from closing the channel, the parking publisher, the scheduler or the worker replaced the exception leaving `close()` or `build()`, and Java discards the replaced one. Each now adds the later failures to it as suppressed, and a step that throws no longer skips the ones after it, so a recovery listener whose removal fails no longer skips the consumer cancel or the wait for a running handler. See [#1112](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1112).
* **The MongoDB lease refresh of a competing consumer keeps running after a round throws an `Error`, and an `OutOfMemoryError` thrown outside a listener no longer loses a lease grant or prohibition.** The round is logged at ERROR and the next one runs as scheduled. In 0.33.0 and 0.34.0 the `Error` stopped every later round on that node with nothing logged, so the node never refreshed a lease it held or took a free one again. An `Error` thrown while one subscription's lease is refreshed also no longer skips the other subscriptions in the same round. A grant or prohibition that an `OutOfMemoryError` cut short, in a refresh round or while the strategy called the listeners, is told by the next round, and so is the prohibition of a release or unregister whose lease removal failed. In 0.34.0 each of those was lost. A `CompetingConsumerListener` can now be told the same thing twice, and be told of a register, release or unregister after it returned, which the breaking change below describes. When the thread that starts or pauses a subscription after a round took or lost its lease can't be started, because of an `OutOfMemoryError` for example, the later rounds try again until the thread starts. In 0.34.0 no later round started or paused it. Fixes [#1349](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1349), see [ADR 156](doc/architecture/decisions/0156-a-refresh-round-tells-the-listeners-what-they-have-not-heard.md).
* **A competing consumer logs a pause that the wrapped subscription model refused at WARN instead of DEBUG.** The line names the subscription, the subscriber and the exception. The lease is still released as before. The DEBUG line was added in 0.34.0.
* **The Kotlin `executeAndReturnState` extensions for a `DcbDecider` refuse a `null` state before appending the command's events.** Both overloads, on the blocking and on the reactor `DcbApplicationService`, appended the events and then threw a `NullPointerException`, and a caller seeing the failure was likely to run the command again. They now throw it before anything is appended. The state type is non-null in Kotlin, so only a decider whose decided state is `null` anyway gets here, such as one written in Java. The extensions are `inline`, so code compiled against 0.34.0 keeps the old behaviour until it's compiled against 0.35.0. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. Resolves [#1122](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1122).
* **`ReactiveSnapshotDeciderApplicationService` deletes a snapshot that is ahead of its stream also when `executeAndReturnState` refuses a `null` state.** In 0.34.0 the refusal came before the check, so after a stream reset without a matching `SnapshotStore.delete`, the stale snapshot stayed for as long as every command was refused. A refused command now makes a write with no events, which persists nothing but returns the stream's current version. The stale snapshot is deleted, and then the `NullPointerException` is thrown. Resolves [#1115](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1115).
* **The javadoc of every `executeAndReturnState` says what happens to a `null` state.** The blocking services return it as it is, and the reactive ones write none of the command's events and fail with a `NullPointerException`, because a `Mono` can't hold `null`. Resolves [#1119](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1119).
* **Kotlin's `dcbProjection { }`, `dcbSingletonProjection { }` and `dcbSnapshotView { }` can now be called without an initial state.** Use that instead of passing `initialState = null` when the first state comes from the first event, as `projection { }`, `singletonProjection { }` and `snapshotView { }` already allowed in 0.34.0. The block and the returned descriptor then see the state as `S?`, so a handler that uses it without a `null` check fails to compile. Resolves [#1022](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1022).
* **A subscription model can declare that it delivers every event of a subscription in commit order, through the new `CommitOrderedSubscriptions` capability.** `deliversInCommitOrder(subscriptionId)` answers `true` only when no event reaches the subscription before every earlier event matching it has been delivered and its listener returned normally. An event is earlier when it committed earlier, or when it was written earlier in the same commit. A delivery whose listener throws is retried in place before anything after it. `deliversEveryEventInCommitOrder()` is `true` only when the model delivers every one of its subscriptions in commit order at every moment, and is `false` unless the model declares otherwise. A wrapper is found only when it implements the capability itself, and a caller that finds nothing treats the model as not in commit order. Saga support will read it. No subscription model declares it yet.
* **The event store TCK gains `DcbLateCommitConformance` and `StreamLateCommitConformance`, for a store that can commit an append after one with a higher position.** Each suite holds one append after its position is assigned and before it commits, lets a later append commit first, and then checks that a decider, a view and a reader resuming from the position they were given still get the held append. The DCB suite also checks that a consistency token read during the hold still refuses a condition the held append matches. A fixture extending either suite overrides the new `EventStoreFixture.lateCommits()`. A store that reserves a position before its transaction commits answers `LateCommits.holding(..)` with a way to hold its next append, and a store that assigns and commits a position in one step answers `LateCommits.inPositionOrder()`. The default throws, so an existing fixture keeps compiling and fails only the suites that need the answer. Part of [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230) and [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **The subscription TCK's `RestartConformance` now checks that a restart delivers an event that committed late, below the position the subscription had already reached.** It checks it when the model resumes by itself after a restart. A new subscription started at a position the application stored itself, `StartAt.checkpoint(GlobalCheckpoint.of(..))`, has to deliver that event too, or be refused, because such a position holds nothing that shows no event committed below it. `RestartableSubscriptionModelFixture` gains three methods. `lateCommits()` is the same hold the event store TCK uses. `honoursAGlobalPosition()` says whether the model starts after a stored global position, and a model that answers `false` has to refuse it with an `UnsupportedStartAtException`. `refusesABareGlobalPosition()` says that the model refuses it with a `CatchupCannotResumeException` instead, as a catch-up does, and then a subscription started at the checkpoint the model delivered with an event has to deliver the late event. The first two throw by default, so a fixture keeps compiling and fails only the restart suite until it answers them. The third answers `false`. The append hold the two TCKs share, `LateCommits` and `HeldAppend`, now lives in `occurrent-tck-common`. Part of [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **A DCB read can resume from marker versions instead of a position, so an append that committed late below a position the reader already saw is no longer skipped.** On MongoDB an append reserves its position before its transaction commits, so a read after position 42 can miss an append at 41 that commits later. `read(criteria, DcbReadOptions.withVersions())` now reports `DcbMarkerVersions`, the epoch and version of each marker of the criteria, and a conditional append reports the same for its condition. `read(criteria, DcbReadOptions.afterVersions(saved))` returns every matching event appended after those versions, whatever its position, and throws `DcbSnapshotInvalidException` when the store can't compare them, for example after the markers were pruned. A full read is the answer then. A read that asks for versions on MongoDB runs in a short read-only transaction of its own, bounded by the new `markerVersionsCommitTimeout` setting, 5 seconds by default, and `resumeFromMarkerVersions(false)` turns resuming off. Every DCB append on MongoDB now also counts its marker increments, gives each marker an epoch and writes the epoch and version of each of its markers into each event, under an `mv` field that a read never returns and that a new index covers. `DcbAppendConfirmation` answers whether an append is committed at majority. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#dcb-reads-can-resume-from-marker-versions), [ADR 148](doc/architecture/decisions/0148-dcb-snapshots-resume-from-marker-versions-not-positions.md) and [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230).
* **A catch-up over a MongoDB event store no longer needs the `hostInfo` command.** It reads its live start, the point where the live subscription takes over, with a `find` on the event store's position counter at majority read concern, so it runs on shared Atlas tiers (M0, Flex) and under a role that only grants `find`. The catch-up used to read it from the subscription model's `globalCheckpoint()`, which still runs `hostInfo` for its other callers. A catch-up falls back to `globalCheckpoint()` only when its event store reports no live start, as the in-memory event store does. On a MongoDB server that isn't a replica set, a catch-up now fails with an `IllegalStateException` saying it needs one, where it used to fail with a `NullPointerException`. See [ADR 155](doc/architecture/decisions/0155-a-catch-up-reads-its-live-start-from-the-event-store-and-refuses-to-resume-where-it-could-skip-an-event.md).
* **A catch-up that runs without the live delegate now goes live from the live start it read before its replay, also when it restarts in the middle of that replay.** The checkpoints its replay stores hold that live start, in a new form that starts with `position:<n>;v:2;` for a position replay and `position:time;v:2;` for a time replay, so an event that commits late, below what the replay already read, is delivered after the restart. A replay that started after position 0 or after the beginning of time stores the new form as well. A catch-up runs without the live delegate when the model it wraps, such as `DurableSubscriptionModel`, answers `null` to its start, as under the Spring Boot starter's `resumeBehavior = SAME_AS_START_AT`. Such a catch-up now always goes live from the live start, and fails with an `IllegalStateException` when neither the event store nor the subscription model reports one. 0.34 refuses the new form, so read [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-catch-up-keeps-its-live-start-in-the-checkpoints-it-stores) before a rolling upgrade or a rollback. Part of [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230) and [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **A catch-up that loses its live start during or right after each of its replays now fails with `CatchupCannotResumeException`.** It extends `IllegalStateException`, which the catch-up threw before, and its message names the longest replay, measured from reading the live start to checking it, so you can size the change stream history, such as the MongoDB oplog, to reach back at least that far. A blocking catch-up whose replay a resume or a takeover started now holds that replay after the failure and logs it once, and doesn't run it again until the subscription is resumed or `start(true)` is called. A catch-up that can only replay by position, a DCB catch-up and every reactor catch-up, throws the same exception when it is asked to start from a time replay's checkpoint in the new form. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#when-a-catch-up-loses-its-live-start-on-every-replay).
* **A time-based catch-up whose `catchupPhaseSortBy` doesn't start with the time ascending no longer stores a time below which it hasn't delivered every event.** During its first read it stores the time it started from, so a restart during that read replays it from the start. Under the default `SortBy.ascending(TIME, STREAM_VERSION)` it stores what it stored before. Part of [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230).
* **An event that a blocking catch-up replays now holds the checkpoint the catch-up stores after it, so an application that keeps that checkpoint with its own state can resume from it.** `StreamCatchupSubscriptionModel` and `DcbCatchupSubscriptionModel` deliver each replayed event as a `CheckpointAwareCloudEvent`, as the live subscription and the reactor catch-ups already did. Read the checkpoint with `EventMetadata.getCheckpoint()` or `DcbEventMetadata.checkpoint()`, and start from it again with `StartAt.checkpoint(new StringBasedCheckpoint(checkpoint))` or the new `DcbStartAt.checkpoint(checkpoint)`. A `CheckpointAwareCloudEvent` equals only another one with the same checkpoint, so a test that compares a replayed event with the `CloudEvent` it wrote now fails, and compares `getOriginalCloudEvent()` instead. `EventMetadata` equality doesn't include the checkpoint, so metadata of the same event from the replay and from live compare equal. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-replayed-event-holds-the-checkpoint-to-resume-from) and [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **A Spring Boot application can say where its annotated subscriptions resume with a `SubscriptionResumePoints` bean.** Spring Boot asks it for each `@Subscription`, `@StreamSubscription`, `@DcbSubscription`, `@Projection`, `@Saga` and `@Snapshot` subscription when it starts, whatever start position the annotation gives. A non-null answer is the checkpoint the subscription resumes from, ahead of the one Spring Boot's `CheckpointStorage` holds, and `null` keeps the annotation's start. Under `resumeBehavior = SAME_AS_START_AT` the answer only moves where the replay starts, and the subscription keeps no checkpoint once it is live. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-replayed-event-holds-the-checkpoint-to-resume-from) and [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **A blocking catch-up that goes live without a replay no longer starts a `DurableSubscriptionModel` that its start answers `null` to.** When the start resolved to the checkpoint of a live event, or to the model default with nothing stored, `StreamCatchupSubscriptionModel` and `DcbCatchupSubscriptionModel` handed the durable model they wrap that resolved start instead, and it stored a checkpoint after each event for a subscription meant to keep none. In 0.34 a `StartAt.dynamic` written against the model API could do this. In Spring Boot it happened when a `SubscriptionResumePoints` bean answered the checkpoint of a live event under `resumeBehavior = SAME_AS_START_AT`. Part of [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **`startAtGlobalPosition` on `@Subscription`, `@Projection`, `@Saga` and `@Snapshot`, `startAtDcbPosition` on `@DcbSubscription`, and `DcbStartAt.afterPosition(long)` are deprecated for removal.** Every value above 0 is now refused at start. Use `StartPosition.BEGINNING` or `DcbStartAt.beginning()`, and resume after an event from the checkpoint the subscription delivers with it, in Spring Boot from a `SubscriptionResumePoints` bean. Part of [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).

#### Breaking changes

* **A saga no longer uses an event's global position to recognise a redelivery.** An event that has a `position` but no `streamid` and `streamversion` is refused under the default `RedeliveryDetection.REQUIRED`. Under `BEST_EFFORT` it is applied with a warning and can't be quarantined, and an unreadable `streamid` or `streamversion` fails the delivery under both settings. A stored `positionWatermark` still loads and is ignored. Events read from an Occurrent event store are not affected. This reverses position-based deduplication that shipped in 0.31.0. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-global-position-is-no-longer-a-saga-redelivery-key), [ADR 150](doc/architecture/decisions/0150-a-global-position-is-not-a-saga-redelivery-key.md) and [#1298](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1298).
* **`RepositionableSubscriptions.findIn(..)` on a blocking `DurableSubscriptionModel` finds the durable model itself, and a resume at a position through it stores the checkpoint before the subscription resumes.** The position is stored only when the wrapped model's new default method `RepositionableSubscriptions.restartsAt(Checkpoint)` answers `true`, which it doesn't unless the model overrides it. The MongoDB subscription models answer `true` for every checkpoint their change stream can open at, and `false` for a `GlobalCheckpoint`, a `CatchupTimeCheckpoint`, a time and every other one. In 0.33.0 and 0.34.0 the lookup found the model under the durable model, which resumed without storing anything, so a restart before the next event started from the older checkpoint. A catch-up model over a durable model now has the position stored when it resumes the durable model at one. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-resume-at-a-position-through-a-durablesubscriptionmodel-stores-the-checkpoint), [ADR 151](doc/architecture/decisions/0151-a-capability-that-acts-on-a-subscription-is-not-found-past-a-wrapper.md) and [#1253](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1253).
* **The blocking `capability(..)` lookup, and the `findIn(..)` methods built on it, no longer look past a `SubscriptionModelWrapper` for a capability that acts on a subscription.** `RepositionableSubscriptions`, `Subscribable`, `CancellableSubscriptions` and the model types built on them are found only on a model that implements them. The capabilities that observe subscriptions, `IntrospectableSubscriptions`, `ReplayAwareSubscriptions`, `HistoryRetainingSubscriptions`, `QuietPositionReportingSubscriptions` and `Pushable`, are still found through a wrapper, and so is `HistoryLossReportingSubscriptions`. So `RepositionableSubscriptions.findIn(..)` on a `CompetingConsumerSubscriptionModel` or a `ManualStartSubscriptionModel` answers empty. In 0.33.0 and 0.34.0 it answered the model beneath, and a resume at a position through that answer ran the subscription while the competing consumer still had it as paused, with no lease competed for. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-capability-that-acts-on-a-subscription-is-not-found-past-a-wrapper), [ADR 151](doc/architecture/decisions/0151-a-capability-that-acts-on-a-subscription-is-not-found-past-a-wrapper.md) and [#1251](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1251).
* **A saga applies a stream version that arrives after a higher one from the same stream.** It used to keep one number per stream, skip every version at or below it, and so lose an event that a broker or a partitioner delivered late, with nothing logged. The saga now keeps the highest applied version per stream and event type, together with the versions it applied below that mark, and applies a late version unless it is `SagaRunnerConfig.reorderWindow()` (256 by default) or more behind the mark. An event behind the window is still skipped, and the saga logs a warning once per stream, unless it failed in the saga. A failed version is recorded as pending on the instance, whether or not quarantine is on, and applied once when it arrives again however far the instance has moved on, which is what makes replaying a parked event safe. A saga instance now stores up to 256 extra numbers per stream and event type, in a new `streamMarks` field. A document without the field reads as before, and a 0.34.0 node can read one that has it. `SagaEnvelope` gains a 14th record component and `SagaRunnerConfig` a 6th, so a record pattern over either stops compiling. The constructors stay. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-saga-applies-a-stream-version-that-arrives-after-a-higher-one), [ADR 154](doc/architecture/decisions/0154-a-saga-tells-a-late-stream-event-from-a-redelivery-per-event-type.md) and [#1305](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1305).
* **The deprecated `accept(..)` is removed from the blocking and the reactor `DomainEventFeed` and `CatchupProjectionFeed`.** Call `acceptAndWait(..)`, which takes the same arguments and returns the same type, and which `accept(..)` already forwarded to. Code that still calls `accept(..)` on one of them stops compiling. Run `UpgradeToOccurrent_0_34` while the project still depends on 0.33.0 or 0.34.0 and it renames the calls, and then move to 0.35.0. On 0.35.0 the recipe no longer finds them, so rename each call by hand. `accept(..)` on `PushSubscriptionModel` is a different method and stays. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#the-deprecated-accept-on-the-projection-feeds-is-removed), [section 12 of the 0.34.0 guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#12-a-projection-feeds-accept-waits-until-the-event-is-applied-and-fails-when-it-is-not), which deprecated it, and [#1276](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1276).
* **A RabbitMQ source stops instead of retrying when it can't tell whether a negative acknowledgement or an acknowledgement went out.** Releasing a held delivery put its tag back after any failure, and a `MetricsCollector` that throws after the frame was sent made the next poll send it again for the same tag, which makes the broker close the channel with `406 PRECONDITION_FAILED`. A release that fails because a dropped connection already closed the channel is made again on the next poll. A release that fails because a `TrafficListener` or a `MetricsCollector` threw, or because the broker closed the channel or the connection has no automatic recovery, stops the source with an `error` log line, and closing its channel puts the delivery back on the queue. An `IOException` from a release or an acknowledgement on a recovering connection stops the source up to about two poll intervals later, unless the connection dropped after the call. In 0.34.0 such an `IOException` never stopped the source. A failed flush to the socket drops the connection, so a source on a `Recoverable` channel keeps running. NIO's `Frame enqueuing failed` on a connection a memory alarm has blocked comes while the connection stays up, so it stops the source. A channel closed for good stops the source on its next poll, also when it holds no delivery. That is a channel the broker closes, a channel the application closes, or whose connection it closes, logged at `warn`, and any close of the client's own channel from a connection without automatic recovery. A source built on a channel that wraps the client's own and isn't `Recoverable` logs a warning when it's built, and a connection close that amqp-client's default recovery-triggering condition accepts doesn't stop it, whether or not the connection under the wrapper recovers, since a recovery there may reopen the channel. If nothing reopens it, the source stays up and consumes nothing. The source stops taking deliveries before it closes the channel, so a delivery the client sends after that starts no handler. A delivery failure after the source has stopped no longer stops it a second time. It is logged at `debug` when the handler was interrupted or `close()` has stopped waiting for it, and at `warn` otherwise, and an interrupt is passed on to the thread. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-rabbitmq-source-consumes-again-after-a-recovery-without-topology-recovery), [ADR 153](doc/architecture/decisions/0153-a-rabbitmq-source-registers-a-new-consumer-when-a-recovery-leaves-it-without-one.md) and [#1107](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1107).
* **The RabbitMQ source removes the recovery listener it adds for its worker when it stops, and two of its log lines say what was sent.** The park line says a copy was parked and the acknowledgement for the original was sent, and the failure line says a negative acknowledgement was sent, since the client ignores an acknowledgement for a tag from before a recovery. A search or an alert on the old text stops matching. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-rabbitmq-source-consumes-again-after-a-recovery-without-topology-recovery) and [#893](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/893).
* **A `CompetingConsumerListener` added to a MongoDB lease strategy can be told the same thing twice, and can be told of a register, release or unregister after that call returned.** In 0.34.0 a listener was told each change once, and a register, release or unregister told the listeners of its own change before it returned. A listener can now be told the current answer again, for example after an `OutOfMemoryError` cut a call short or when a lease is lost while the strategy works out what to tell. It can also be told a prohibition with no grant before it. A listener that throws an `Error` is told the current answer again in the next round, and in every round after that until it stops throwing, except that when the `Error` came on a prohibition and the same call then granted a new lease, only the grant is told again. A register, release or unregister may call the listeners before it returns, and throw what they throw, but only for the change it made itself and only while nothing else is calling them about that consumer, and anything else they are told, which can include that change, comes on a notifier thread, before or after the call returns, where what they throw is logged. So a listener's `Error` that came out of `subscribe`, `resume` or `cancel` in 0.34.0 can now end up only in the log, at ERROR. `CompetingConsumerSubscriptionModel` handles all of this, so only a listener of your own needs a look. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-competingconsumerlistener-can-be-told-the-same-thing-twice-and-after-the-call-returns), [ADR 156](doc/architecture/decisions/0156-a-refresh-round-tells-the-listeners-what-they-have-not-heard.md) and [#1349](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1349).
* **`executeAndReturnState` on `ReactiveSnapshotDeciderApplicationService` and `ReactiveSnapshotDcbDeciderApplicationService` returns `Mono<@NonNull S>`.** The state type is still `S extends @Nullable Object`, so a decider whose state is `null` until it's created can still be passed, and the emitted state is declared non-null, which the method already enforced at runtime. Kotlin code that wrote the result type by hand as `Mono<State?>` stops compiling, so drop the `?`. The reactor `DcbDeciderApplicationService` gets the same signature, which lets Kotlin pass a decider with a nullable state type, and breaks nothing. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#the-reactive-executeandreturnstate-emits-a-non-null-state) and [#1119](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1119).
* **`DcbAppendResult` and `DcbEventStream` gain a component, `Optional<DcbMarkerVersions> markerVersions`, and `DcbReadOptions` gains two, `versionsRequested` and `afterVersions`.** Every existing constructor stays, and sets the new components to empty. An `isEqualTo(...)` on a whole result a MongoDB or in-memory store returned now fails when the store reported versions, so compare the components you mean. A record pattern with the old number of components stops compiling, and `UpgradeToOccurrent_0_35` adds the missing bindings. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#dcb-reads-can-resume-from-marker-versions), [ADR 148](doc/architecture/decisions/0148-dcb-snapshots-resume-from-marker-versions-not-positions.md) and [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230).
* **A catch-up subscription model now refuses an event store it can't read through one session, when you construct it.** `StreamCatchupSubscriptionModel`, `DcbCatchupSubscriptionModel` and their reactor counterparts throw an `IllegalArgumentException` that names `CatchupReads` unless the `EventStoreQueries`, `PositionOrderedReader` or `DcbEventStore` you pass is an event store that implements it. The MongoDB and in-memory event stores do. A decorator around one, a custom `EventStoreQueries` bean and a test double do not, and a Spring Boot application whose event store bean is such a decorator fails at startup. Hand the catch-up the event store itself and decorate around the subscription instead. The catch-up replays from the live start it reads in that session, and a store it can't read that way would let it skip an event that commits late. The `CatchupReads` types are internal, in packages named `internal`, and are not public API. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-catch-up-needs-the-event-store-itself) and [ADR 155](doc/architecture/decisions/0155-a-catch-up-reads-its-live-start-from-the-event-store-and-refuses-to-resume-where-it-could-skip-an-event.md).
* **A catch-up model that holds both a stream and a DCB catch-up now refuses a subscription with no filter that starts at a position checkpoint.** The blocking `CatchupSubscriptionModel` and `ReactorCatchupSubscriptionModel` throw an `IllegalArgumentException` before they subscribe anything. With no filter they can't tell which catch-up should restore the position, and routed it to the DCB one, so a stream subscription restored that way skipped its stream events. Pass a `StreamSubscriptionFilter`, a `DcbSubscriptionFilter` or an `AgnosticSubscriptionFilter`. The broker `CloudEventForwarder.forward(subscriptionId, startAt)` and `DomainEventForwarder.forward(subscriptionId, startAt)` pass no filter, so they are refused too when you start them at a position checkpoint over such a model. Under a `CompetingConsumerSubscriptionModel` the subscribe throws when the node gets the lease straight away or the competing consumer model is stopped. A node that gets the lease later logs a warning, gives the lease back, and fails the same way at every later grant. A time start, a beginning start and a model with only one catch-up are not affected. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-catch-up-over-both-a-stream-and-a-dcb-model-refuses-a-position-start-without-a-filter).
* **`DcbStartAt` gains a case, `DcbStartAt.AtCheckpoint`, which `DcbStartAt.checkpoint(String)` returns.** `DcbStartAt` is sealed, so a `switch` over it that lists every case and has no `default` no longer compiles until it handles the new one. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-replayed-event-holds-the-checkpoint-to-resume-from) and [#1297](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1297).
* **A catch-up now refuses a start after a position above 0 that holds no live start.** On MongoDB an event can commit below a position a reader has already read past, and a catch-up started after that position with no live start read before it never delivered that event. `startAtGlobalPosition` and `startAtDcbPosition` above 0, `DcbStartAt.afterPosition(n)`, `StartAt.checkpoint(GlobalCheckpoint.of(n))` and a stored `position:<n>` that holds no live start now throw `CatchupCannotResumeException`, and so does a stored `TimeBasedCheckpoint` with no live start that a blocking catch-up resumes from, which it used to resume from with a warning. A start at position 0 or at the beginning is not refused. On an event store that writes no positions, a blocking stream catch-up throws `UnsupportedStartAtException` instead for `StartAt.checkpoint(GlobalCheckpoint.of(n))` above 0, as the next entry describes. To resume after an event, keep the checkpoint the subscription delivers with it. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-catch-up-refuses-a-start-that-could-skip-an-event-committed-below-it). Part of [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230).
* **A blocking stream catch-up over an event store that writes no positions now replays the history for a start at position 0, and refuses a start above 0.** `StreamCatchupSubscriptionModel` handed `StartAt.checkpoint(GlobalCheckpoint.of(n))` to the MongoDB subscription model without a replay, which opened the change stream at the present and skipped every event before it with no error. The blocking Spring Boot starter starts `startAt = StartPosition.BEGINNING` on `@Subscription`, `@Projection`, `@Saga` and `@Snapshot` at position 0, so such a subscription got none of the history when `occurrent.event-store.stream.position` was `false`, or when the store had turned stream position off for itself because it started against a collection that already held events without a `position`. Position 0 now replays from the beginning of time, and a position above 0 throws `UnsupportedStartAtException` from `subscribe(..)`. A subscription that went live that way under the default `resumeBehavior` resumes from the checkpoint it stored and still doesn't get the history, so delete that checkpoint with the application stopped. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-stream-catch-up-over-an-event-store-without-positions-replays-from-position-0).
* **A catch-up whose replay started above position 0 or after the beginning of time now fails the first time it loses its live start.** That includes a catch-up from a specific time, such as `StartAt.checkpoint(TimeBasedCheckpoint)` or a time start in Spring Boot. It used to replay again from where it started, up to three times, with a live start read then, which skipped an event below that point written after the lost live start was read. It throws `CatchupCannotResumeException` instead, which also refuses a 0.34 checkpoint whose live start the change stream history no longer reaches after the application was down for the upgrade. A replay from position 0 or from the beginning of time still replays again, up to three times. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-catch-up-refuses-a-start-that-could-skip-an-event-committed-below-it). Part of [#1230](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1230).
* **A MongoDB subscription model now refuses a subscribe at a `GlobalCheckpoint` or a `CatchupTimeCheckpoint`.** `NativeMongoSubscriptionModel`, `SpringMongoSubscriptionModel` and `ReactorMongoSubscriptionModel` opened the change stream at the present for the checkpoints a catch-up stores, and skipped every event before it with no error. Their `subscribe(..)` now throws an `UnsupportedStartAtException`, and the reactor model's unnamed `subscribe(..)` fails its `Flux` with it. Only a `StartAt` that names the checkpoint is refused. A `StartAt.dynamic(..)` that answers one still opens the change stream at the present. A blocking `DurableSubscriptionModel` does that when it reads one from its storage for a dynamic start, which [#1346](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1346) tracks. A resume at such a checkpoint still goes on from the position the subscription has read to, and the Spring Boot starters, which put a catch-up in front of the MongoDB model, are not affected. See [the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.35.0.md#a-mongodb-subscription-model-refuses-a-catch-ups-checkpoint) and [#1336](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1336).

### 0.34.0 (2026-10-23)

#### Highlights

* Occurrent can forward stored events to a broker at least once, through a `CloudEventSink` you write yourself or the new RabbitMQ and Kafka modules. Those modules also consume from the broker into a push subscription or a projection.
* A broker listener acknowledges a message based on the `RoutingOutcome` that `acceptOrDefer(..)` returns, on both stacks. An optional `PushObserver` is told the same outcomes.
* Several fixes stop a subscription from silently skipping events. A position catch-up restarted in the middle of its replay no longer misses an event that committed late, and a time-based catch-up no longer misses an event with an earlier time that commits during its replay. A `ReactorMongoSubscriptionModel` subscription that starts at the present no longer misses an event written right after `subscribe(..)` returns. A MongoDB subscription that starts at the present no longer skips events when it is paused or restarted before its first event.
* MongoDB subscriptions save their position even when no events match. A subscription that was quiet for a long time now resumes where it was after a pause, a restart or a lease moving to another node, as long as it was stopped for less than the oplog window.
* Declaring a sealed event supertype now matches every concrete type it permits in projections, subscriptions, queries, snapshot views, `ExecuteFilter` and `DcbCriteriaBuilder`, as it already did for sagas.
* A saga instance that keeps failing can be quarantined on the event it stopped on, instead of failing forever. Quarantine is off by default.
* Events that `updateEvent` damaged in 0.33.0 or earlier can be repaired with the new `occurrent-eventstore-mongodb-update-event-repair` module, and a store warns at startup when it finds any.

#### Changes

##### Brokers

* **Occurrent can forward stored events to a broker at least once, with the new `occurrent-broker-api-blocking` module.** `CloudEventForwarder` runs a durable subscription from its default start position and hands each stored event to a `CloudEventSink` you write. The checkpoint moves only once the sink returns, so a sink that throws gets the same event again on the next run. That holds as long as your sink doesn't return before the broker has taken the event. `DomainEventForwarder` does the same for domain events. The two `CloudEventForwarder` overloads that take an explicit start position don't resume, as their javadoc explains. See [ADR 133](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md). Resolves [#413](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/413), [#414](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/414), [#415](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/415), [#416](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/416), [#417](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/417), [#419](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/419), [#846](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/846), [#867](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/867) and [#922](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/922).
* **The new `occurrent-broker-rabbitmq-blocking` module publishes to and consumes from RabbitMQ.** `RabbitMqCloudEventSink` and `RabbitMqDomainEventSink` publish, and a publish returns only once RabbitMQ has confirmed and routed the message. A routing key with no binding fails the publish rather than losing the event. `RabbitMqCloudEventSource` and `RabbitMqDomainEventSource` consume, and their javadoc covers threading and connection recovery. `example/broker/rabbitmq` runs it end to end.
  * `build()` retries opening the channel and declaring the queue, 100 ms up to 2 seconds over ten attempts, so a broker still starting doesn't fail startup. Change that with `retryStrategy(RetryStrategy)` or `occurrent.broker.rabbitmq.source.retry.*`.
  * `close()` waits up to `closeTimeout(Duration)`, 30 seconds by default (`occurrent.broker.rabbitmq.source.close-timeout`), and puts every delivery it didn't finish back on the queue, even if shutting down throws.
  * A RabbitMQ source's queue and its bindings must exist before the first subscription starts on its model, or before `catchUp`, `catchUpAll` or `goLive` is called on its `DomainEventFeed`. `build()` creates them unless you set `declareTopology(false)`, so build the source first, and with `declareTopology(false)` create them first yourself. An event published after that point and before they exist never reaches the queue, and the publish still succeeds when another queue matches its routing key. The javadoc of `RabbitMqCloudEventSource` has the detail.
* **The new `occurrent-broker-kafka-blocking` module publishes to and consumes from Kafka.** `KafkaCloudEventSink` and `KafkaDomainEventSink` return once the broker acknowledges under `acks=all`, which the sink sets when your config doesn't, and refuses to start with anything weaker. `KafkaCloudEventSource` and `KafkaDomainEventSource` consume, and refuse to start unless `enable.auto.commit` is `false`. Their javadoc says what a failed record or a stop does to each partition. A partition the group has no committed offset for, or whose committed offset the topic no longer holds, starts at the oldest record the topic still holds unless you set `auto.offset.reset` yourself. A new group then doesn't miss an event published while it joins, and a group whose committed offsets expired or were deleted reads every record the topic still holds again.
  * `KafkaCloudEventMapper` reads `streamversion` and `position` back as `Long`, and keeps data that is present but empty, fixing two defects in the `cloudevents-kafka` reader.
* **Kafka sends every event to one shared topic by default, keyed by stream id, so a stream stays in order across event types.** That is `KafkaSharedTopicDestinationResolver`. Pick the topic's partition count before producing to it, since growing it later can break that order. `KafkaTopicPerTypeDestinationResolver` gives each event type its own topic instead, ordered only within that type.
* **`RabbitMqDomainEventSink` and `KafkaDomainEventSink` publish the same extensions from `publish(E)` and `publish(EventMetadata, E)`.** Both strip only `streamid`, `streamversion`, `position` and `appendid`, which an event that was never stored can't have, and publish every other extension the converter set. Where `metadata` names the same key, `metadata` wins.
* **`acceptOrDefer(CloudEvent)` returns the event's `RoutingOutcome` on both push models, so a broker listener acknowledges on the outcome.** The reactor `PushSubscriptionModel` gets a new `acceptOrDefer(CloudEvent)` returning a `Mono<RoutingOutcome>`. `accept(CloudEvent)` is unchanged, and returns normally for an event fed before any subscription is registered, while the model is stopped, or while the subscription is paused, so a listener that acknowledged on return lost those events. A filter or handler failure throws on the blocking stack and errors on the reactor stack, as with `accept(..)`. Resolves [#1138](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1138). See [section 13 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#13-only-the-in-memory-event-stores-write-path-may-feed-a-push-models-accept).

  | Outcome | What happened | `disposition()`, and what a listener does |
  |---|---|---|
  | `DELIVERED` | A running, unpaused subscription's filter accepted the event. | `ACKNOWLEDGE`. Acknowledge it. |
  | `FILTERED` | That subscription evaluated the event and declined it. | `ACKNOWLEDGE`. Acknowledge it. |
  | `UNAVAILABLE` | No running, unpaused subscription to reach, a paused one included. | `HOLD`. Have the broker deliver it again, as the javadoc of `RoutingOutcome.Disposition.HOLD` describes, and don't park it. |
  | `DEFERRED` | A `CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel` in front is still replaying, and refused the event instead of buffering it. On the reactor stack it also reports a replay holding back an event the handler wrote while handling the event. | `HOLD`. Have the broker deliver it again, as the javadoc of `RoutingOutcome.Disposition.HOLD` describes, and don't park it. |
  | `NOT_DELIVERABLE` | A filter threw, or the catch-up refused the event for a reason that can pass, such as a full live buffer. On the reactor stack it also reports a call from that subscription's own handler, which is refused every time, since the event could only be queued there, and the catch-up refusing an event the handler wrote while handling the event, after the handler ran. | `FAIL`. Apply your delivery failure policy. |
  | `REFUSED` | The refusal is permanent, as for every event once a catch-up has failed. | `STOP`. Stop consuming. |

  `mayAcknowledge()` answers the acknowledge question on its own, so a source you write yourself switches on four `disposition()` values rather than six constants.
* **Every shipped source acknowledges a broker message only when the event was actually handled.** Each acts on the `RoutingOutcome` from `acceptOrDefer(..)`, as the table under `acceptOrDefer(CloudEvent)` above shows. A message the table marks `HOLD` skips your `DeliveryFailurePolicy`, `PARK` included. A `FAIL` message is redelivered (`REDELIVER`, the default), or published to a parking destination and acknowledged once that is confirmed (`PARK`), set with `onDeliveryFailure(DeliveryFailurePolicy)`. See [ADR 133](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md).
  * A source declares its own queue and bindings unless you set `declareTopology(false)`. `bindingFilter(...)` takes a `Filter` or a `SubscriptionFilter`. An explicit empty `bindings(...)` fails `build()` with `IllegalStateException`, on Kafka always and on RabbitMQ when the source declares its topology.
* **A source can pause pulling messages while a catch-up in front of it replays, which cuts down redeliveries.** Wire `readinessSource(catchupThenPush::isReadyForLiveDelivery)` on `RabbitMqCloudEventSource` or `KafkaCloudEventSource`. It only paces the source, since the `RoutingOutcome` of each message is what keeps it safe. Both starters wire it for you for a `@Projection` or `@Saga` with `source = PUSH`. A second `@Projection` or `@Saga` on the same `PushSubscriptionModel` bean in the same application context fails at startup, also under `occurrent.subscription.mode = manual`, where the source would otherwise ask the wrong catch-up and stay paused. See [the readiness amendment](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md#amendment-2026-08-21-one-evaluation-replaces-the-readiness-gate-for-catch-up-subscriptions).
* **Push models take an optional `PushObserver` that is told the `RoutingOutcome` of every event `accept(...)` was asked to deliver.** Before, a wrong queue binding, a missing declared event type or a type-mapping typo looked the same as a saga or projection that chose not to react. Pass the observer through the constructor that takes a `DataFieldReader`. The default, `PushObserver.noop()`, changes nothing and skips the match check entirely. The outcomes are in the table above, and `PushObserver`'s javadoc says when each one is reported on each stack. Resolves [#802](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/802), [#848](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/848) and [#1116](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1116).
  * Anything your observer throws is logged and never changes what the model does with the event, except an `Error` other than `AssertionError`, which reaches the caller.
* **`DomainEventFeed` gains `acceptOrDeferCloudEvent(CloudEvent)` and `acceptAndWaitCloudEvent(CloudEvent)` on both stacks, for a broker source that holds a `CloudEvent` rather than a decoded domain event.** Both match the event against the `Filter` given to `register(...)` and decode only a match with the feed's `CloudEventConverter`. `acceptOrDeferCloudEvent` returns the `RoutingOutcome`, and a matching event that arrives before the projection is live returns `DEFERRED`. `acceptAndWaitCloudEvent` waits like `acceptAndWait(..)` for a matching event, and returns straight away for one the filter doesn't match. `refusesPermanently()` turns `true` once the projection's own catch-up has failed, and never turns back. Resolves [#848](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/848).
  * An optional `DataFieldReader` constructor argument lets the feed answer a payload condition live. One it can't answer throws `UnreadableLiveFilterException` on the first `acceptOrDeferCloudEvent(...)` or `acceptAndWaitCloudEvent(...)` call rather than on `register(...)`, and the same exception instance on every later call. See [ADR 133's amendment](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md#amendment-2026-08-18-a-domain-level-payload-filter-is-refused-on-first-live-match-use-not-at-register-and-the-refusal-is-permanent) for why it refuses there.
* **The new `occurrent-broker-rabbitmq-spring-boot-starter` and `occurrent-broker-kafka-spring-boot-starter` configure the two brokers in Spring Boot.** There is one starter per broker, so neither broker client ends up on the other's classpath. Turn one on with `@EnableOccurrentRabbitMqBroker` or `@EnableOccurrentKafkaBroker`. Each builds a sink and a source factory from `occurrent.broker.rabbitmq.*` or `occurrent.broker.kafka.*`, with the builder defaults, so you name only the queue or consumer group per source. The source factories are `RabbitMqCloudEventSourceFactory` and `KafkaCloudEventSourceFactory`, each with a domain-event twin.
  * The RabbitMQ starter needs a `com.rabbitmq.client.Connection` bean of your own, and does nothing until one exists. `spring-boot-starter-amqp` doesn't provide one, so declare it yourself.

##### Sagas

* **A saga instance that keeps failing can be quarantined instead of failing forever. Quarantine is off by default.** Turn it on with `SagaRunnerConfig.defaults().withQuarantineAfter(Duration.ofMinutes(5))`, or with `occurrent.saga.quarantine-after` on the annotation path. An instance that has failed for longer than that can move to `SagaStatus.QUARANTINED`, and then applies no further events and fires no timers. Nothing in this release brings it back, so `SagaStateStore.delete(sagaId)` is the only way out. See [section 8 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#8-a-saga-instance-that-keeps-failing-can-be-quarantined-and-four-saga-types-change-with-it) and [the quarantined saga runbook](doc/runbooks/quarantined-saga-instance.md). Resolves [#818](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/818), [#997](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/997), [#1042](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1042), [#1061](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1061) and [#952](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/952).
  * Each `@Saga` now also publishes its running `SagaSubscription` as a `sagaSubscription-<id>` bean.
* **A saga timer reaction that throws an `Error` no longer makes the instances after it in that poll miss their turn.** A `StackOverflowError` or a `NoClassDefFoundError` from one instance used to abandon the rest of the batch, on every poll. An `OutOfMemoryError` still abandons the poll. A batch is at most `SagaRunnerConfig.timerBatchLimit` instances, a hundred by default, and if that many instances keep failing the saga can still stop firing timers altogether, which [#1003](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1003) is fixing. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.32.0. See [ADR 134](doc/architecture/decisions/0134-a-saga-instance-that-keeps-failing-is-quarantined-at-its-own-position.md) and [#998](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/998).
* **A flow saga's `stepWindow` now only counts the events its steps declare, plus the type that starts the flow.** An event of any other type, which a wide `replacementFilter` or a `CloudEventTypeMapper` that maps several types onto one can let in, no longer evicts one of the step's own events. It is still kept, so a step fed only such events has no `stepWindow` cap. Most flows need no change. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [ADR 129](doc/architecture/decisions/0129-a-flow-sagas-stepwindow-caps-only-its-own-declared-events.md) and [section 2 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#2-a-flow-sagas-stepwindow-now-caps-its-declared-events-and-the-start-type). Resolves [#773](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/773), [#764](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/764) and [#1047](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1047).
* **A flow saga instance waiting in a step you renamed or removed now fails with an `IllegalStateException` that says what to do, instead of a `NullPointerException`.** Put the step back, or add a temporary step under the old name that moves its instances on, or delete the stuck instance from the `SagaStateStore`. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [ADR 128](doc/architecture/decisions/0128-a-renamed-or-removed-step-refuses-its-parked-instances.md) and [#748](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/748).
* **`SpringMongoSagaStateStore` retries a transient MongoDB error instead of failing the saga with it.** Every read and write, and the two indexes it creates when built, back off from 100 ms up to 2 seconds and give up after 10 attempts with the original failure. Before, such an error counted against the quarantine budget and could quarantine a healthy instance. This applies whether you build the store or the Spring Boot starter does. Pass your own `RetryStrategy` to the new five-argument constructor, or `RetryStrategy.none()` to turn retrying off. Resolves [#1001](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1001).
* **`SagaFilters` is now public, as `org.occurrent.dsl.saga.internal.SagaFilters` in `dsl/saga-dsl/common`.** It builds the filter a saga subscribes with, like the already public `ProjectionFilters`, so a future reactive saga runner can reuse it. It was package-private in `dsl/saga-dsl/blocking`, so nothing breaks. Resolves [#786](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/786).

##### Event types and stored events

* **A declared sealed event type now matches every concrete type it permits, wherever Occurrent builds a filter from it.** That is projections, subscriptions built through the subscription DSL, `DomainEventQueries`, `@Snapshot` views, `ExecuteFilter`'s `type` and `includeTypes`, and `DcbCriteriaBuilder`'s `type` and `types`. Before, each matched only the supertype's own CloudEvent type and silently missed events. Sagas and annotation-based subscriptions got this in 0.33.0. An enum whose constants have bodies is expanded into its constant classes too. See [ADR 126](doc/architecture/decisions/0126-every-derived-event-type-filter-expands-a-declared-sealed-type.md) and [section 3 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#3-declaring-an-event-type-whose-concrete-subtypes-cannot-be-found-is-refused). Resolves [#750](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/750), [#758](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/758), [#912](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/912), [#939](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/939) and [#940](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/940).
* **`ExecuteFilter.excludeTypes` on a sealed type now excludes every concrete subtype it can find.** `excludeTypes(SensitiveEvent.class)` used to exclude only `SensitiveEvent`'s own CloudEvent type, which no event is stored under. Where a class that is neither sealed nor final reopens the hierarchy, the exclusion can still let events through, so seal the hierarchy or declare the concrete types. See `EventTypeExpansion.expandWhatCanBeFound` and [section 3 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#3-declaring-an-event-type-whose-concrete-subtypes-cannot-be-found-is-refused). Resolves [#912](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/912).
* **`updateEvent` no longer lets an update function lose or change the values the store owns, on every event store Occurrent ships.** Those are `position`, `streamId`, `streamVersion` and the DCB tags, and the event `updateEvent` returns now matches the stored one. Events damaged before this release need a one-off repair, see [section 10 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#10-events-updateevent-damaged-before-0340-need-a-one-off-repair). Fixes [#876](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/876), [#904](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/904), [#905](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/905), [#906](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/906) and [#927](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/927).
  * `InMemoryEventStore` dropped `position`, `streamId` and `streamVersion`, which could make `read()` throw a `NullPointerException`, and it stored a forged `streamVersion` as given, so `read(originalStream).version()` could return it.
  * `DcbCloudEvents.preserveTags`, `OccurrentCloudEventExtension.preservePosition` and `preserveStreamIdentity` join `preserveAppendId`. The TCK's `EventStoreOperationsConformance` and `DcbStreamInteropConformance` now check all of this.
* **A new module, `occurrent-eventstore-mongodb-update-event-repair`, repairs events that `updateEvent` damaged before 0.34.0.** It restores `position` and the `dcbTags` array from what the document still holds, and lists by `_id` the events it can't repair. Upgrade every writer to 0.34.0 before you run it. A store that writes `position` now warns at startup when it finds a damaged one. The repair doesn't move consumer checkpoints, so step 7 of the runbook uses the position range in `UpdateEventRepairResult` to find consumers that read past a repaired event. See [the repair runbook](doc/runbooks/update-event-repair.md) and [section 10 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#10-events-updateevent-damaged-before-0340-need-a-one-off-repair).
* **`EventStoreConfig.Builder.requireRepairedEvents(true)` stops a MongoDB event store from starting while it holds events that `updateEvent` damaged in 0.33.0 or earlier.** A damaged event is missing from the conflict check behind a conditional append, so an append that should be refused is accepted. Turn it on if you'd rather have the application down. The check reads the whole collection, and can keep refusing after a repair until you fix the leftovers by hand as step 5 of the [repair runbook](doc/runbooks/update-event-repair.md) describes. It is off by default on the native, Spring blocking and Spring reactor stores, which then warn about a string `position` and start. See section 4 of [ADR 136](doc/architecture/decisions/0136-a-store-reports-damage-it-caused-and-never-repairs-it-by-itself.md). Resolves [#973](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/973).
* **`ReactorMongoEventStore.updateEvent` no longer logs the whole document it found, data included, at INFO.** `updateEvent` is the operation you'd use for GDPR-style redaction, so logging the payload there defeated the purpose. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [#907](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/907).
* **Reading a stream with a `StreamReadFilter` and a skip now skips by stream position, on every event store Occurrent ships.** The skip used to count only the events the filter matched, so `ApplicationService#execute` with both `ExecuteOptions.fromStreamVersion(n)` and a filter could resume from the wrong place. A negative skip now throws `IllegalArgumentException` on every store, and the shared conformance suite checks it. See [#810](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/810).
* **The native MongoDB store no longer leaks a `MongoCursor` when you close a stream read's `Stream` early**, for example with Kotlin's `.use { }`. The Spring store already closed it. See [#810](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/810).
* **`exists(DcbCriteria)`, `exists(streamId)` and `exists(Filter)` on the native `MongoEventStore` now stop at the first matching event.** They used to count every match, apart from `exists(Filter.all())` outside a transaction, so a `true` answer got slower the more events matched.
* **A MongoDB event store with `DCB` and without `STREAM` no longer creates the `dcbTags_1` index.** On a collection of only DCB events it held the same events as `position_1` and cost an index entry per tag on every append. A store with both `STREAM` and `DCB` still creates it, on the native, Spring blocking and Spring reactor stores. A DCB-only store whose collection also holds stream events with a `position` warns at startup when it has no usable index on `dcbTags`, and the warning has the command that fixes it. See [ADR 145](doc/architecture/decisions/0145-the-standalone-dcbtags-index-is-for-match-all-dcb-queries.md) and [section 27 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#27-a-dcb-only-mongodb-event-store-no-longer-creates-the-dcbtags-index). Resolves [#1233](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1233).

##### Spring Boot

* **`@Transactional` and other Spring aspects on a `@Subscription`, `@StreamSubscription`, `@DcbSubscription` or `@SynchronousSubscription` handler now apply to every delivery, also during startup.** These handlers now register once every singleton exists, like `@Projection`, `@Snapshot` and `@Saga`. A handler method Spring's proxy can't call now fails startup instead of running without advice. A handler on a bean built later, a `@Lazy` bean for example, registers when the bean is built, and one Spring's proxy can't call fails the creation of that bean instead of startup. See [section 11 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#11-a-subscription-handler-spring-cannot-invoke-is-now-refused-and-a-live-subscription-can-miss-a-startup-write) and [ADR 127 section 4](doc/architecture/decisions/0127-a-subscription-is-a-descriptor-and-the-annotation-stops-naming-the-concept.md#4-a-descriptor-annotation-is-read-after-the-singletons-are-instantiated). Resolves [#837](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/837) and [#965](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/965).
  * A proxy that a `BeanPostProcessor` registered after Occurrent's adds later, and that can't reach the handler method, fails on the first delivery rather than at startup.
* **A `@Projection`, `@Saga` or `@Snapshot` factory method no longer fails startup when its bean is a JDK interface proxy.** Occurrent now unwraps the proxy and calls the factory on the bean behind it. When that bean's class isn't exactly the class the scan found the method on, as with a prototype-scoped factory bean that builds a different class on a later call, it refuses with `IllegalStateException` instead of running the wrong code. See [ADR 127 section 4](doc/architecture/decisions/0127-a-subscription-is-a-descriptor-and-the-annotation-stops-naming-the-concept.md#4-a-descriptor-annotation-is-read-after-the-singletons-are-instantiated). Fixes [#836](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/836).
* **A `@Projection`, `@Saga` or `@Snapshot` on a bean built after startup now registers when the bean is built. Before, it never registered.** That covers a `@Lazy` bean, and a `FactoryBean` product or `@Bean` method that declares an interface while the annotation is on the class behind it. On the reactor stack such a bean can be built on a non-blocking thread. There a handler that starts at the beginning or an explicit position subscribes on `Schedulers.boundedElastic()` and retries a failure that can go away, while one that starts at `NOW` or `DEFAULT` subscribes on the calling thread. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [ADR 127 section 4](doc/architecture/decisions/0127-a-subscription-is-a-descriptor-and-the-annotation-stops-naming-the-concept.md#4-a-descriptor-annotation-is-read-after-the-singletons-are-instantiated) and [#981](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/981).
* **`ManualStartPushSources` no longer keeps a push `@Projection` or `@Saga` running past the close of the application context.** A start after the close began is refused, or what it started is stopped. Before, a saga's timer poller could keep sending commands into the closed context, and a projection could replay against a store being shut down. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. Resolves [#988](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/988), [#1020](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1020), [#1027](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1027), [#1034](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1034) and [#1036](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1036).
  * A projection fed by a `DomainEventFeed` is refused before it registers, since a feed can't unregister it and would buffer live events until the buffer overflowed into your publish path.
  * With `catchup = NONE`, the subscription on your own `PushSubscriptionModel` bean is now cancelled at close, also under `occurrent.subscription.mode = auto`. It only stayed behind when that bean outlived the context.
  * Under `startupMode = BACKGROUND`, an id in the list `startAll()` returns means its replay is running, not finished. A start that reads the shutdown flag just before the close sets it can still register, see [#1032](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1032).
* **A `@Projection(source = PUSH)` fed by a `DomainEventFeed` no longer fails startup because the `CheckpointStorage` refuses its id.** Such a projection never uses `CheckpointStorage`, but the starter still checked its id, so a storage that refuses some ids, like `SpringRedisCheckpointStorage` in its Cluster-safe mode, could stop startup. A feed bean the starter can't identify without creating it is still checked. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [section 8 of the 0.33.0 upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.33.0.md#8-three-ways-the-spring-boot-starter-now-refuses-to-start) and [#788](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/788).
* **`MigrateSubscriptionModeProperty_0_32` no longer rewrites the wrong profile in a multi-document `application.yml`.** One profile matching its YAML recipes made them rewrite every profile in the file. A profile that never set `occurrent.subscription.mode` could lose its `occurrent.subscription.enabled` key, and its whole document if that was all it had. A profile whose `enabled` held the other boolean got that boolean written under `occurrent.subscription.mode`, which only takes `disabled`, `manual` or `auto`. `DropRedundantYamlProperty` and `MigrateSubscriptionEnabledInYaml` now check each YAML document on its own. The recipe ids are unchanged, and `.properties` files were never affected. Fixes [#828](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/828) and [#834](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/834).

##### Projection feeds and push subscriptions

* **`CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel` writes its catch-up marker only while it still owns the subscription id.** An attempt whose id was cancelled writes nothing, so a marker that exists always means the history was read in full, and a restart that finds it skips the history. Neither stack writes the marker while the model is stopped. On the blocking stack a slow checkpoint store no longer holds up `stop`, `start`, `pauseSubscription` and `resumeSubscription`. See [ADR 133's amendment](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md#amendment-2026-08-22-a-catch-up-marker-means-the-ids-history-has-been-read-and-every-later-attempt-trusts-it).
* **`CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel.cancelSubscription(..)` deletes the catch-up marker on both stacks, so subscribing the same id again replays the history.** Before, the marker stayed, and the new subscription went straight to live events, also after a restart. On the blocking stack a failed delete makes `cancelSubscription(..)` throw with the subscription still running, and you can call it again. On the reactor stack the delete runs after the call returns. Wait for the `Mono` it now returns if a restart must not skip the history, and call `cancelSubscription(..)` again if it fails. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.32.0. See [section 23 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#23-a-reactor-cancelsubscription-returns-a-mono-that-completes-once-the-stored-state-is-deleted) and [ADR 133's amendment](doc/architecture/decisions/0133-a-broker-is-a-transport-for-the-push-feed-and-never-a-subscription-model.md#amendment-2026-09-30-a-cancel-deletes-the-catch-up-marker).
* **A new `AppliedAppendStore` tells you whether a projection has applied a given append, so a caller can wait to read its own write.** `waitUntilApplied(projectionId, appendId, timeout)` polls until it has or the timeout passes. Set `@Projection(recordAppliedAppends = true)` to record automatically on both stacks, or call `recordApplied` yourself. Both Spring Boot starters configure a MongoDB-backed store under `occurrent.projection.applied-append`. See [ADR 132](doc/architecture/decisions/0132-an-append-has-an-identity-and-read-your-writes-becomes-a-membership-question.md). Part of [#740](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/740), [#865](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/865) and [#871](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/871).
  * `true` means the projection has applied the first event of that append it handles, not necessarily every event of it.
  * `recordAppliedAppends = true` fails startup without an `AppliedAppendStore` bean, or with `mode = SYNCHRONOUS`. The reactive MongoDB store's `waitUntilApplied` throws `IllegalStateException` on a non-blocking thread, so call it from `Schedulers.boundedElastic()`.
* **A projection that records applied appends also records an append it only received from a catch-up replay.** That covers all six catch-up-then-live compositions, both `CatchupProjectionFeed`s, both `DomainEventFeed`s and both `CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel`s. It records up to 10,000 appends per catch-up, and a wait for one past that times out. An event your `Projection.id` returns `null` for records nothing. `CatchupThenLiveOptions.dedupCacheSize` now sizes two caches, so each catch-up-then-live registration holds up to twice as many ids. See [ADR 137](doc/architecture/decisions/0137-a-live-payload-the-replay-already-delivered-still-reaches-its-source.md), and [ADR 132 decision 8](doc/architecture/decisions/0132-an-append-has-an-identity-and-read-your-writes-becomes-a-membership-question.md#8-the-owner-of-a-composition-says-which-model-to-listen-to) for wiring a recording projection by hand. Part of [#963](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/963).
* **`CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel` matches the replay and live copies of an event by CloudEvent id and source together, on both stacks.** It used the id alone, so when two producers each sent an event with id `1`, the replay delivering one of them stopped the other from being delivered live. Part of [#1041](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1041).
* **`goLive()` after `stopCatchUp()` on a `CatchupProjectionFeed` or `DomainEventFeed` delivers the live copy of an event the stopped replay already delivered.** A view that buffers during a replay throws the buffer away on a stop, so it lost that event for good. A view that writes straight through now gets it twice, which `goLive()`'s at-least-once delivery allows. On the reactor stack, `goLive()` after a finished `catchUp()` also keeps the feed accepting events, where it used to refuse every later one. Part of [#1041](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1041), resolves [#974](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/974) and [#1153](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/1153).
* **`catchUp()` on a feed that is already live no longer loses the live events that arrive while it replays.** It holds them back until the replay ends, and delivers them whether the replay completes or is stopped. On the reactor stack the feed also keeps delivering after that stop, where it used to drop every later event. `stopCatchUp()` on either stack still stops a running replay when a `catchUp()` follows it, where 0.33.0 let that call undo a stop the replay hadn't noticed yet.
* **A reactor projection feed or push subscription answers an event only once the events its fold or handler fed while applying it are applied too.** This covers the reactor `CatchupProjectionFeed`, `DomainEventFeed` and `CatchupThenPushSubscriptionModel`. In 0.33.0 a broker listener got its answer once the fold returned, while an event the fold had fed was only queued, so the listener acknowledged the message and a crash before the fed event was applied lost it. Now the `Mono` from `acceptAndWait(..)` or `PushSubscriptionModel.accept(..)` waits for those events, and for the events their folds feed in turn, and errors when one of them fails or is refused. The event is then not recorded as applied, so the broker delivers it again and it is applied again. An event fed more than `CatchupThenLiveOptions.maxBufferedEvents()` levels below the listener's event, each fed from the fold of the one above, is refused. Fixes behaviour shipped in 0.33.0. See [section 15 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#15-feeding-a-reactor-catch-up-from-its-own-handler-no-longer-waits-forever).
* **A reactive `@Projection(source = PUSH)` fed by a `DomainEventFeed` into a `MaterializedView` gets each live event's real `EventMetadata`, not `EventMetadata.empty()`.** A projection keyed on the stream id or reading `appendid` never worked in that combination. The starter now uses the metadata-aware `reactiveUpdateWithMetadata` there, as every other reactive registration already did. Part of [#740](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/740).

##### Subscription models

* **`DurableSubscriptionModel` records a new subscription's start position in `subscribe(..)`, and refuses the subscription when it can't.** This hits a subscription from `StartAt.subscriptionModelDefault()` with no checkpoint stored, on a server that refuses the `hostInfo` command, such as a shared MongoDB Atlas cluster. `subscribe(..)` then throws `IllegalStateException`. Up to 0.33.0 it started anyway, and a crash before the first checkpoint lost the event whose delivery had failed. To start anyway, set `occurrent.subscription.start-when-no-start-position-can-be-recorded=true`, or `startWhenNoStartPositionCanBeRecorded(true)` on `DurableSubscriptionModelConfig` or `ReactorDurableSubscriptionModelConfig`. See [section 7 of the upgrade guide](doc/migration/upgrading-to-0.34.0.md#7-durablesubscriptionmodel-refuses-a-first-subscription-when-no-start-position-can-be-recorded) and [#852](https://github.com/johanhaleby/occurrent/issues/852).
* **`DurableSubscriptionModel` writes a subscription's first checkpoint with an `ifAbsent` condition and the same fallback for a lost race as `ManualStartSubscriptionModel` and `ReactorDurableSubscriptionModel`.** Before, it read the checkpoint and then saved over whatever it found. On a checkpoint storage that evaluates write conditions, with no lease protecting the write, a second node could overwrite the first node's position. Every setup Occurrent ships puts a lease outermost, so this makes the models consistent rather than closing a race you could run into. See [ADR 130](doc/architecture/decisions/0130-a-subscriptions-first-position-race-resolves-by-order-not-by-write-order.md).
* **On MongoDB, two nodes recording a manually started subscription's first checkpoint at the same time no longer lose the events between their two positions.** `CheckpointStorage` gains an opt-in `resolveFirstCheckpointRace`, which the native and Spring MongoDB storages implement so the earlier position wins, whichever write reached storage first.
